# Checking Faithful Acceleration Result

**Summary.** Abram Demski and Claude conduct a detailed technical review of disputed claims about logical induction and faithful acceleration. The conversation explores whether a theorem (ingredient II: Expectation Unbiasedness From Feedback) holds universally or only within an admissible domain, focusing on a problematic quote-referencing diagonal (g_n) where an agent's calibration claim may fail. Claude develops an adjudication plan and explicit counterexamples to audit the proof's logical gaps, working toward independent verification of the scope.

**Where to look:**
- 1-2: User requests Claude read four technical documents (deference, faithful-acceleration, scope note, pointwise-tower). Claude summarizes all four files and notes that recommended edits have not been applied to source files yet.
- 3-4: User states goal to adjudicate the debate about faithful acceleration. Claude proposes structured plan with 6 steps to understand dispute.
- 5-6: User requests detailed written plan document for reference during long conversation. Claude flags potential gap in scope note's §4 step 3 and plans comprehensive write-up.
- 7-8: User asks for clear explainer of both the main claim (bounded ε-violation universally) and the counterclaim (fails on quote-referencing diagonal), written in style similar to faithful-acceleration.md §5.

An **EF-progression** is defined inside Definition 3.4.3 (Expressible Feature), p. 17 (main.tex ℓ786–793), leaning on the rank notion from Definition 3.4.1 (Valuation Feature), same page (ℓ767–770). The chain is: a *valuation feature* is "a continuous function from valuation sequences to real numbers such that $\alpha(\overline{\mathbb V})$ depends only on the initial sequence $\mathbb V_{\le n}$ for some $n \in \mathbb N^+$ called the *rank* of the feature"; $\mathcal F_n$ is the features of rank $\le n$, and an $\mathcal F$-progression is "a sequence of features such that $\alpha_n \in \mathcal F_n$" (all in 3.4.1). *Price features* $\phi^{*n}(\overline{\mathbb V}) := \mathbb V_n(\phi)$ are Definition 3.4.2. Then 3.4.3: "An **expressible feature** $\xi \in \mathcal F$ is a valuation feature expressible by an algebraic expression built from price features $\phi^{*n}$ …, rational numbers, addition, multiplication, $\max(-,-)$, and a 'safe reciprocation' function $\max(1,-)^{-1}$. … We write $\mathcal{EF}$ for the set of all expressible features, $\mathcal{EF}_n$ for the set of expressible features of rank $\le n$, and define an **$\mathcal{EF}$-progression** to be a sequence $\overline\xi$ such that $\xi_n \in \mathcal{EF}_n$."

So an EF-progression is a sequence of these algebraic price-expressions whose $n$-th member may reference prices only up through day $n$ — including day $n$'s own prices, which is why continuity matters (it's what keeps same-day dependence well-defined at the market-clearing fixed point). The "e.c." in Definition 4.3.5 then applies to the sequence of *expressions*: a poly($n$)-time machine (Def. of e.c., ℓ753–754; p. 14) outputs the $n$-th expression, and the weight $q_n$ is that expression's value on the actual market. The source's own footnote is a good gloss on the design (ℓ788): "expressible features are a generalization of arithmetic circuits. The specific definition is somewhat arbitrary; what matters is that expressible features be (1) continuous; (2) compactly specifiable in polynomial time; and (3) expressive enough to identify a variety of inefficiencies in a market." Fuller treatment with examples is Appendix A.2.

Incidentally, this is the definition doing the work in two places in the review document: rank-$\le n$ is the previsibility backbone of "generable", and the poly($n$) generator budget is exactly what §5.8's ledger-visibility point turns on — the expression's rational constants are the only channel by which non-price facts enter, and the generator can only compute poly($n$)-cheap ones.

The example's engine is the size arithmetic, which the prose states quietly ("clusters of size 1, 10, 100, 1000, …", ℓ1155; published p. 27–28): each cluster is 10× everything before it, so the newest cluster is always ~90% of all days so far. The running frequency of truth after cluster $k$ is therefore $\approx 0.9\,b_k + 0.1\,(\text{old average})$, where $b_k$ is the parity of $\pi[\mathrm{Ack}(k,k)]$ — so the frequency swings to within ~10% of each new cluster's value, forever, with no decay. That's what "correlated clusters" is doing: not correlation per se, but perfect correlation *at a growing scale that outpaces averaging*. Constant-size clusters would restore frequency convergence across clusters and there'd be no example. Meanwhile the market's prices sit at 50% (the right marginal belief, given Ackermann-digit hardness), so the uniform-weighting bias ratio $\approx \tfrac12 - \text{freq}_n$ oscillates around $\pm 0.4$, passing through 0 mid-cluster as each new block's mass grows — which is why the calibration passage says the reasoner "only appears well-calibrated on the rare days where it crosses 50%" (ℓ1162), and why Recurring Unbiasedness's conclusion is exactly consistent: the crossings *are* the limit point at 0.

Given that, I read the remark as doing two jobs at once. First, it's the **sharpness witness for 4.3.6**: it exhibits (modulo the π-hardness assumption) a sequence where the bias ratio genuinely doesn't converge on the uniform weighting, so "0 is a limit point" can't be strengthened to "→ 0" without new hypotheses. Second — and this is the focus I think you're asking after — it's the **witness that closes route (a) and forces route (b)**. The fork was announced back at the start of §4.3 (ℓ1170): "we can recover some good calibration properties … if we either (a) restrict our consideration to sequences where the average frequency of truth converges; or (b) look at subsequences … where $\mathbb P$ has 'good feedback'". The paragraph right before the remark completes route (a): decompose by any generable weighting into frequency-convergent pieces. The remark then says clusters defeats (a) in every direction available to a generable weighting: separating true-clusters from false-clusters requires predicting the parities ("(most likely) no polynomial-time algorithm can predict the jumps", p. 30) — and generability's *extra* power over plain e.c., price-dependence, buys nothing here because the prices are flat at ½ precisely because nobody can predict (a self-consistent fixed point: if price-gates could separate, the market would already have moved). Fuzziness likewise manufactures no signal. What poly-time index-dependence *does* give you is the cluster boundaries — which is enough for the one selection that works: sample sparsely, one representative per cluster, waiting until the previous sample's digit is computable before the next. That selection is exactly a deferral-image-supported weighting with good feedback, i.e., Definition 4.3.7 and Theorem 4.3.8 are reverse-engineered from what survives of the example. The same example then drives §4.4's definitional choice (ℓ1293): benford's pseudorandomness excludes clusters (frequency diverges), so they broaden it to frequency-convergence on "independent subsequences where the $n{+}1$st element of the subsequence doesn't come until after the $n$th element can be decided" — the $f$-patient machinery — and *that* is how the paper finally answers "how does $\mathbb P$ perform on clusters": price → 50%, correctly.

Two things the remark is *not* focusing on, which I flag because they're easy misreadings: it isn't claiming the inductor is defective on clusters — the 50% price is the right answer, and the limit-point guarantee still holds on every generable weighting; the problem is entirely about which *stronger theorem* is provable, not about behavior. And the correlation in play is temporal (values tied across neighboring indices, with the hardness borrowed from π), not correlation with the market's own prices — the reflexive case lives in §4.11 with a different character: there the impossibility of separating the sides is *forced* by continuity and sub-expressible margins, whereas clusters' undecomposability is a plausibility claim resting on a number-theoretic hardness assumption. That contrast is worth keeping in view given our adjudication: this remark is the paper's own articulation of the currency distinction that did all the work there — full-limit unbiasedness is purchased either by frequency convergence or by feedback-sparse supports, and by nothing else on offer.

So my one-sentence answer to "what is it focusing on": the example is tuned so that *dense* selections fail for size-growth reasons and *value-based* selections fail for computational reasons, leaving sparse-with-feedback as the unique survivor — the remark is the pivot that turns that survivor into the next definition. Does that land on the thing you were unsure about, or was your uncertainty more about why they chose temporal correlation rather than some other obstruction to decomposability?

The reference resolves to Garrabrant, Soares & Taylor 2016, "Asymptotic Convergence in Online Learning with Unbounded Delays", arXiv:1604.05280 (LI bibliography, p. 75). I pulled its source and read it. The first thing to notice is that its central counterexample environment is literally the stochastic twin of $\overline{\mathrm{clusters}}$, down to the index arithmetic: the environment $P^{\#}$ "flips a fair coin and reveals it once, and then flips another and reveals it ten times, then flips a third and reveals it one hundred times, and so on", with new coins at indices $2, 12, 112, 1112, \ldots$ — and the observations "contain no information about the coin currently being predicted: the forecasters must predict the $k$th coin all $10^{k-1}$ times before it is revealed." The LI paper derandomizes this with parities of Ackermann digits of $\pi$; the delays paper keeps it stochastic precisely so that "the 'correct' probabilities a bounded reasoner should assign" are well-defined (0.5), sidestepping the question of what probabilities are right for deterministic facts.

The paper then proves the negative result that grounds everything: under unbounded delays with correlated blocks, *dense* evaluation of forecasters is meaningless. The Bayes-optimal forecaster $f^*(\cdot) = 0.5$ accumulates loss at rate $\tfrac14$, while the "gambler" $f^1 \equiv 1$ recoups everything whenever a giant cluster lands heads — since the newest cluster is ~90% of history, the gambler's total loss swings below $f^*$'s about half the time, forever. Against both gamblers $f^1$ and $f^0$, "the only way for a forecaster to avoid high regret … is for it to place higher probability on the true result of the coin every single time", which fails infinitely often with probability 1 — so consistency (average regret $\to 0$) is *impossible*, and even the genuinely optimal predictor looks arbitrarily bad on dense comparisons. They also close the obvious escape: a decaying learning rate tuned to neutralize $10^k$-clusters is destroyed by $P^{\#}_2$ with $10^{10^k}$-clusters, and per-environment tuning "would be an ad-hockery tantamount to hardcoding the optimal forecaster in from the beginning". That last point is, in LI terms, exactly why one quantifies over *all* deferral functions rather than fixing one patience profile.

The positive result is where the broadening gets its justification. Diagnosis, in their words: "the gamblers are abusing the fact that they can correlate many predictions before any feedback on those predictions is received, so we can foil the gamblers by assessing them only on a subsequence of predictions where each prediction in the subsequence was made only after receiving feedback on the previous prediction in the subsequence." Formally, $s$ is **independent** if $o_{s_i}(s_{i-1})$ is defined — the previous pick is revealed before the next prediction. The proof of their main theorem shows why this is exactly the right condition and not just a sufficient one: on an independent subsequence, the comparison against a Bayes-optimal forecaster becomes a *martingale* — they build the alternating chain $G_1 H_1 G_2 H_2 \ldots$ (information at prediction time, then the reveal), the first-order residual $r$ satisfies $\mathbb E[r(H_i) \mid G_i] = 0$ by Bayes-optimality, its swing is bounded by the disagreement term $v$, and an Azuma-type concentration lemma separates optimal from non-optimal experts with probability 1 (with a clever $\rho\varepsilon/2m^2$ handicap so that "the longer a contender waits to prove itself, the more it has to prove"). Without independence, a whole cluster's residuals are one correlated lump, the per-step conditional-expectation-zero structure is gone, and — by the impossibility half — no repair exists. So independent subsequences are simultaneously *sufficient* (their algorithm EvOp converges to Bayes-optimal behavior on every such subsequence) and *maximal* (anything denser is provably noise). The price is stated plainly: the subsequences "might be very sparse", and the convergence bounds are "very, very weak".

The bridge to "pseudorandom" is their §5. They invoke Martin-Löf's characterization — a random sequence satisfies every computably-checkable property that holds with probability 1 for randomly generated sequences — and propose the resource-bounded analog: random *with respect to a class* $\mathcal F$ iff it satisfies every such property checkable by members of $\mathcal F$, while admitting "the authors do not yet know of a satisfactory definition of algorithmic randomness with respect to resource constraints." Now look at $P^{\#}$ through that lens: sampled one-per-cluster with reveals in between, the picks are literally i.i.d. fair coins, so the frequency converges to $\tfrac12$ on *every* independent subsequence by the law of large numbers — all of the correlation lives in the intra-block structure that independence discards. So the LI paper's Definition 4.4.2/4.4.3 move — count a sequence as pseudorandom when frequency converges on deferral-independent ($f$-patient) subsequences — is the pullback of the ordinary LLN-based notion along exactly the tests that remain meaningful under unbounded prediction-to-settlement delays. Demanding more (frequency convergence on dense subsequences) would exclude clusters and give no guarantee; demanding this yields the strong theorem that the market's price matches the frequency (LI 4.4.4 and its LUV version), which certifies 50% on clusters as the *right* answer rather than a calibration failure — the same verdict the delays paper's $f^*$ analysis delivers stochastically.

The dictionary, compactly: their "independent subsequence" is LI's support-in-image-of-$f$ with values computable by the next firing; $f$-patience (bounded mass per deferral window) is the fuzzy relaxation — boundedly much outstanding, not-yet-resolved commitment at any time, rather than exactly one; their gambler impossibility is the reason Recurring Unbiasedness stops at limit points on dense weightings; their $P^{\#}_2$ argument is the reason the definitions quantify over all deferral functions. And the two papers share a hedge at the same joint: LI's "(most likely) no polynomial-time algorithm can predict the jumps" and their unformalized resource-bounded randomness are the same open notion viewed from the two sides — which also matches what we kept finding in the adjudication: the asymptotic, rate-free character of the feedback theorems isn't incidental; even in the clean stochastic model, eventual optimality with feeble bounds is what the structure of the problem affords. The extracted source is sitting in my workspace; say the word if you'd like it copied to outputs for your own reading.

## [43] Claude — 2026-07-17T21:04:58.395950Z


Both questions bite, and the first one bites exactly where the proof said it would. One convention nuance first: your training-run picture — AI equilibrates against frozen human data, then humans see the frozen AI — gives the *human* same-round sight of the quote; the staleness sits entirely on the AI's side, whose calibration instruments can only reference yesterday's human. That asymmetric version is enough to break the unconditional theorem, I think, and in an interpretable way. The failure mode: split the true violation days (quote high, today's human credence low) by yesterday's credence. Days where yesterday's credence was *also* low are catchable — the stale gate fires and the trader's purchase is cheap by definition. The uncatchable days are those where the human's credence *dropped* into violation on the same day the quote fired: no A-generable weighting can concentrate on them, because doing so means predicting the drop from stale data, and the drop can be timed by the deductive-process feed to be unpredictable one day ahead (to both sides — feed $D_H$ the price-moving evidence only on the trigger day). So there is a candidate separation: a coupled pair, consistent with both criteria, in which violations recur forever, always riding same-day surprises. I'd put ~0.6 on that construction going through — it needs checking that the drop pattern doesn't create a one-day-ahead $H$-internal arbitrage, which is exactly what timing-by-fresh-evidence is designed to avoid. What provably survives staleness is a *relative* theorem, and its derivation is two lines on top of §3: run the stale-gated trader with a same-day price check; the forecaster-side average covers the stale gate, the trader realizes it only on the confirmed subset, and the mismatch is bounded by the mass of fired days where the price jumped away. Conclusion shape: for every stale-legal gate, the surprise-free violation weight is either finite or asymptotically dominated by the same-day-surprise weight on fired days (~0.8). Philosophically that seems like the right theorem for your scenario: an AI certified against frozen human opinion can underwrite trust only up to the measured mass of same-day human belief movement — and that surprise mass is observable to the human ex post, so the guarantee degrades gracefully along a quantity the trusting party can monitor. Block-alternation (whole training runs) is the same statement with "same-day" replaced by "within-block", degrading with within-block variation.

On trusting the future AI: the formalization that avoids reference-to-the-unseen is *anticipated* deference — compare the human's current credence in $X$ not with a future quote it hasn't seen, but with its current *expectation of* that future quote. There's a positive chain here whose pieces we already hold. The future quote is a determined LUV of the coupled run, so the human's own Recurring Unbiasedness makes its expectations of future quotes track the realized quotes on average along schedules; realized quotes are dominated by the human's then-credences (Corollary 2, or its scheduled form); and Expected Future Expectations collapses the human's expectation of its own future credence back to its current credence. Chained: the human's expectation of any future quote is, on average and one-sidedly, at most its current credence plus $o(1)$ — the human already anticipates deferring, coherently, without waiting. Sketch-level ~0.7; the step needing care is keeping the three averages on common weightings, which is the same bookkeeping discipline as §3, and the per-day version would need Theorem 2's machinery run on the future-quote LUVs.

The negative side of the future-trust question comes for free from the same sources as before. The diagonal construction doesn't care *which* AI's quote it references: a sentence arranged to be true iff the future AI's quote about it is at most $\tfrac12$ pins that future quote at $\tfrac12$ by the identical margin-gate argument, so trust in the future AI is also only average, one-sided, and content-free on self-referential targets — the reflective ceiling transfers across time rather than being escaped by it. And the two questions compound: a realistic pipeline of generations inserts a fresh staleness layer at every training freeze, so the honest cross-generational statement is presumably the relative, surprise-conditional form iterated — trust in AI$_{k+1}$ conditional on the human-opinion movement during each intervening freeze — which starts to look less like a theorem about equilibria and more like a conservation law for how much certification frozen data can carry forward.

If you want one of these developed properly, my ordering would be the alternation impossibility construction first: it's the sharpest cut (it decides whether joint clearing is a modeling convenience or a substantive idealization of "same-day mutual transparency"), the candidate construction is concrete, and both outcomes reshape the relative theorem's status — as the best-possible result or as a waypoint. The anticipated-deference chain is second: mostly assembly, but the assembly is where this project keeps finding its errors, which by now I take as a reason to do it in writing rather than a reason to trust the sketch.
